### Question 009: Gallbladder and Biliary Tract Pathology: Focus 01-09 (are not radiopaque. Complications include cholecyst)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis and histopathological features of gallstones (cholelithiasis), specifically cholesterol stones, and differentiating them from other types of gallstones based on their composition and associated risk factors. (理解胆结石（胆石症）的发病机制和组织病理学特征，特别是胆固醇结石，并根据其成分和相关风险因素与其他类型的胆结石区分。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old woman with a history of obesity, type 2 diabetes mellitus, and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of severe right upper quadrant abdominal pain radiating to her right shoulder blade. The pain began approximately 2 hours ago after a fatty meal. Her vital signs show a temperature of 37.8°C (100.0°F), heart rate of 110 bpm, blood pressure of 130/80 mmHg, and respiratory rate of 18 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals marked tenderness to palpation in the right upper quadrant with a positive Murphy's sign. Laboratory tests show a slightly elevated white blood cell count (12,000/µL) and mildly elevated liver enzymes (AST 45 U/L, ALT 50 U/L, alkaline phosphatase 110 U/L, total bilirubin 1.2 mg/dL). An abdominal ultrasound reveals multiple echogenic foci within the gallbladder lumen, with posterior acoustic shadowing, consistent with gallstones. One stone is large (approximately 2 cm) and appears to be lodged in the cystic duct. A subsequent cholecystectomy is performed. Gross examination of the gallbladder shows multiple stones filling the lumen, ranging in size from a few millimeters to 2 cm. The stones are predominantly yellow-white and gritty. Microscopic examination of the stones reveals numerous cholesterol crystals embedded in a matrix of mucin, bile pigments, and calcium salts.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism contributing to the formation of these gallstones?

(A) Increased biliary secretion of conjugated bilirubin leading to precipitation of calcium bilirubinate.
(B) Decreased hepatic secretion of cholesterol leading to supersaturation of bile with cholesterol.
(C) Increased biliary secretion of cholesterol coupled with decreased secretion of bile salts, leading to supersaturation of bile with cholesterol.
(D) Increased bacterial activity within the gallbladder leading to deconjugation of bilirubin and precipitation of calcium bilirubinate.
(E) Decreased biliary secretion of mucin leading to increased cholesterol crystal aggregation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old woman with obesity, type 2 diabetes, and hyperlipidemia. These are classic risk factors for cholesterol gallstones (the "Fs": Female, Forty, Fat, Fertile).
- **Symptoms:** Acute RUQ pain radiating to the shoulder (biliary colic), onset after a fatty meal, positive Murphy's sign. These are typical symptoms of symptomatic cholelithiasis, often due to cystic duct obstruction.
- **Labs:** Mildly elevated WBC and liver enzymes suggest inflammation (cholecystitis) but not significant biliary obstruction (bilirubin is only slightly elevated).
- **Imaging:** Ultrasound confirms gallstones (echogenic foci with acoustic shadowing) and cystic duct obstruction.
- **Gross Pathology:** Multiple yellow-white, gritty stones. This appearance is characteristic of cholesterol stones.
- **Microscopic Pathology:** Cholesterol crystals embedded in a matrix. This confirms the composition of the stones.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying mechanism of formation for *these* specific stones (cholesterol stones).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Cholesterol Gallstone Formation:** The primary mechanism involves the supersaturation of bile with cholesterol. Bile is a complex fluid containing water, electrolytes, bile salts, phospholipids (primarily lecithin), and cholesterol. Cholesterol is normally kept in solution by the detergent action of bile salts and phospholipids, which form micelles.
- **Supersaturation:** When the concentration of cholesterol in bile exceeds the solubility limit (typically around 11-13 mg/dL), cholesterol can precipitate out of solution and form crystals.
- **Nucleation:** These cholesterol crystals then aggregate, forming larger stones. Nucleation inhibitors (like apolipoprotein A-I) normally prevent this, but their deficiency can contribute to stone formation.
- **Stasis:** Gallbladder stasis (impaired emptying) allows more time for cholesterol to precipitate and crystals to aggregate.
- **Risk Factors:**
    - **Increased Cholesterol Secretion:** High cholesterol diet, obesity, metabolic syndrome, diabetes, estrogen therapy (pregnancy, oral contraceptives).
    - **Decreased Bile Salt Secretion:** Liver disease, ileal resection (bile salts are reabsorbed in the ileum), rapid weight loss, prolonged fasting, total parenteral nutrition.
    - **Decreased Phospholipid Secretion:** Less common.
    - **Gallbladder Stasis:** Pregnancy, rapid weight loss, prolonged fasting, TPN, diabetes.
- **In this case:** The patient's risk factors (obesity, diabetes, hyperlipidemia) strongly suggest cholesterol stones. The description (yellow-white, gritty, cholesterol crystals) confirms this. The mechanism is therefore increased cholesterol secretion and/or decreased bile salt secretion leading to supersaturation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Increased biliary secretion of conjugated bilirubin leads to the formation of *pigment* stones (specifically black pigment stones), often associated with chronic hemolysis or cirrhosis. These stones are typically dark brown or black, not yellow-white. The patient's presentation and stone description do not fit pigment stones.
- (B): Decreased hepatic secretion of cholesterol would lead to *less* cholesterol in bile, making supersaturation *less* likely. This would decrease, not increase, the risk of cholesterol gallstones.
- (D): Increased bacterial activity leading to deconjugation of bilirubin and precipitation of calcium bilirubinate is the mechanism for *brown* pigment stones, typically found in the biliary tree, often associated with biliary infection (e.g., choledocholithiasis, parasitic infections). These are not the type described in the vignette.
- (E): While mucin is part of the matrix of cholesterol stones and can contribute to crystal aggregation, decreased biliary secretion of mucin is not the primary driving force behind cholesterol stone formation. The fundamental issue is cholesterol supersaturation. Decreased mucin might even facilitate crystal aggregation, but the primary defect is the imbalance between cholesterol, bile salts, and phospholipids.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Cholesterol Stones                                  | Black Pigment Stones                          | Brown Pigment Stones                          |
|----------------------|----------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|
| **Composition**      | Primarily cholesterol (>50%), mucin, bile pigments | Primarily calcium bilirubinate (>20%)          | Primarily calcium bilirubinate, bacteria, mucin |
| **Color**            | Yellow-white, gritty                               | Dark brown/black                               | Brown, soft, greasy                            |
| **Radiopacity**      | Often radiolucent (may have small calcifications) | Often radiopaque (due to calcium)             | Often radiolucent                               |
| **Mechanism**        | Cholesterol supersaturation, stasis                | Increased unconjugated bilirubin (hemolysis, cirrhosis) | Bacterial infection (beta-glucuronidase)       |
| **Associated Risks** | Obesity, female, age, rapid weight loss, diabetes, hyperlipidemia | Chronic hemolysis, cirrhosis                  | Biliary infection, parasitic infection        |
| **Location**         | Gallbladder (most common)                          | Gallbladder, bile ducts                        | Bile ducts (most common)                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The formation of cholesterol gallstones is primarily driven by the supersaturation of bile with cholesterol, often due to increased cholesterol secretion or decreased bile salt secretion, coupled with gallbladder stasis. Key risk factors include the "Fs" (Female, Forty, Fat, Fertile) and conditions like obesity, diabetes, and hyperlipidemia. Differentiate cholesterol stones (yellow-white, gritty, cholesterol crystals) from pigment stones (black - hemolysis/cirrhosis; brown - infection), which have different compositions and mechanisms.

<a id='question-010'></a>

### Question 010: Gallbladder and Biliary Tract Pathology: Focus 01-10 ((calculi within the biliary tract), biliary tract )
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis of pigment gallstones, specifically black pigment stones, and their association with hemolytic anemias leading to increased unconjugated bilirubin. (理解色素性胆结石，特别是黑色素性胆结石的病理发生机制，以及它们与溶血性贫血导致未结合胆红素增加的关系。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old African American woman with a history of sickle cell anemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, constant right upper quadrant abdominal pain that started 2 hours ago. The pain radiates to her right shoulder blade and is associated with nausea. Her vital signs show a temperature of 37.8°C (100.0°F), heart rate of 110 bpm, respiratory rate of 18 bpm, and blood pressure of 130/80 mmHg. Physical examination reveals right upper quadrant tenderness and guarding, with a positive Murphy's sign. Laboratory results show a hemoglobin of 7.5 g/dL (normal range: 12-16 g/dL), MCV of 105 fL (normal range: 80-100 fL), reticulocyte count of 15% (normal range: 0.5-2.5%), total bilirubin of 3.0 mg/dL (normal range: 0.3-1.2 mg/dL), direct bilirubin of 0.5 mg/dL (normal range: 0-0.3 mg/dL), AST of 80 U/L (normal range: 10-40 U/L), ALT of 70 U/L (normal range: 7-56 U/L), and alkaline phosphatase of 150 U/L (normal range: 40-120 U/L). An abdominal ultrasound reveals multiple echogenic foci within the gallbladder lumen, consistent with gallstones, and mild gallbladder wall thickening. Given her underlying condition and the clinical presentation, which of the following is the most likely composition of her gallstones?

(A) Primarily cholesterol crystals with minimal pigment
(B) Primarily calcium bilirubinate and calcium soaps
(C) Primarily cholesterol crystals with a significant pigment component
(D) Primarily calcium bilirubinate and calcium soaps, with a high concentration of calcium phosphate
(E) Primarily cholesterol crystals with a high concentration of calcium phosphate

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old African American woman with sickle cell anemia. Sickle cell anemia is a chronic hemolytic anemia.
*   **Presentation:** Acute right upper quadrant pain radiating to the shoulder, nausea, fever, tachycardia, positive Murphy's sign. This is classic cholecystitis, likely due to gallstones obstructing the cystic duct.
*   **Labs:**
    *   Low Hemoglobin (7.5 g/dL) and high Reticulocyte count (15%) confirm chronic hemolysis.
    *   High MCV (105 fL) is consistent with macrocytosis often seen in hemolytic anemias due to increased reticulocytes.
    *   Elevated Total Bilirubin (3.0 mg/dL) with predominantly Indirect/Unconjugated Bilirubin (Direct = 0.5 mg/dL, Indirect = 2.5 mg/dL). This indicates increased bilirubin production due to hemolysis, overwhelming the liver's conjugation capacity.
    *   Mildly elevated AST, ALT, and Alkaline Phosphatase suggest biliary inflammation/obstruction.
*   **Imaging:** Ultrasound confirms gallstones (echogenic foci) and gallbladder wall thickening (cholecystitis).
*   **Question:** Asks for the likely composition of the gallstones in this patient with chronic hemolysis (sickle cell anemia).

The key link is the chronic hemolysis in sickle cell anemia leading to increased unconjugated bilirubin. Unconjugated bilirubin is poorly soluble in aqueous solutions. In the gallbladder, bacteria (like *E. coli*) deconjugate bilirubin glucuronides, releasing free unconjugated bilirubin. This free bilirubin can then precipitate with calcium ions to form calcium bilirubinate, a major component of black pigment stones. These stones are typically associated with chronic hemolytic states.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Hemolysis:** Sickle cell anemia causes chronic red blood cell destruction (hemolysis).
*   **Bilirubin Metabolism:** Hemoglobin breakdown releases heme, which is converted to biliverdin and then to unconjugated bilirubin. Unconjugated bilirubin is lipid-soluble and transported bound to albumin.
*   **Liver Conjugation:** The liver conjugates bilirubin with glucuronic acid (via UGT1A1 enzyme) to form conjugated bilirubin, which is water-soluble and excreted in bile.
*   **Gallbladder Environment:** In the gallbladder, conjugated bilirubin can be deconjugated by bacterial beta-glucuronidases, releasing free unconjugated bilirubin.
*   **Pigment Stone Formation:** Unconjugated bilirubin is poorly soluble and precipitates with calcium ions (Ca2+) to form insoluble calcium bilirubinate. This is the primary component of black pigment stones. Other components include calcium soaps (calcium salts of fatty acids) and mucin glycoproteins.
*   **Cholesterol Stones:** Cholesterol stones form when bile becomes supersaturated with cholesterol, often due to increased cholesterol secretion or decreased bile salt secretion. They are the most common type of gallstones in Western populations but are less common in patients with chronic hemolysis.
*   **Black vs. Brown Pigment Stones:** Black pigment stones are typically found in the gallbladder and are associated with chronic hemolysis (e.g., sickle cell anemia, hereditary spherocytosis, thalassemia). Brown pigment stones are typically found in the bile ducts and are associated with biliary tract infections (e.g., *E. coli*, *Ascaris lumbricoides*) where bacterial beta-glucuronidases deconjugate bilirubin in the ducts.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Primarily cholesterol crystals with minimal pigment. Incorrect. While cholesterol stones are common, this patient's chronic hemolysis strongly predisposes her to pigment stones due to increased unconjugated bilirubin. The high indirect bilirubin level supports this.
- (C): Primarily cholesterol crystals with a significant pigment component. Incorrect. While mixed stones exist, the dominant pathology in chronic hemolysis is pigment stone formation due to the excess unconjugated bilirubin. The clinical context points strongly towards pigment stones being the primary type.
- (D): Primarily calcium bilirubinate and calcium soaps, with a high concentration of calcium phosphate. Incorrect. Calcium bilirubinate and calcium soaps are components of pigment stones, but calcium phosphate is more characteristic of *brown* pigment stones, often found in the bile ducts in the setting of infection, not typically the primary composition of *black* pigment stones found in the gallbladder due to hemolysis.
- (E): Primarily cholesterol crystals with a high concentration of calcium phosphate. Incorrect. This combines the incorrect primary component (cholesterol) with a component more typical of brown pigment stones. The patient's hemolysis makes cholesterol stones unlikely to be the primary type.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Cholesterol Stones                                  | Black Pigment Stones                               | Brown Pigment Stones                               |
| :------------------ | :-------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Component** | Cholesterol monohydrate                             | Calcium bilirubinate, calcium soaps, mucin          | Calcium bilirubinate, calcium soaps, calcium phosphate |
| **Location**        | Gallbladder (most common)                           | Gallbladder                                        | Bile ducts                                         |
| **Association**     | Obesity, female sex, rapid weight loss, statins, age | Chronic hemolysis (sickle cell, spherocytosis, etc.) | Biliary infection, parasitic infection              |
| **Color**           | Yellowish-white                                     | Black, brittle                                      | Soft, greasy, brown/tan                             |
| **Radiopacity**     | Often radiolucent (unless calcified)                | Often radiopaque (due to calcium)                   | Often radiopaque (due to calcium phosphate)         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Chronic hemolytic anemias (like sickle cell anemia) lead to increased production of unconjugated bilirubin. This excess bilirubin precipitates with calcium in the gallbladder to form black pigment gallstones, composed primarily of calcium bilirubinate and calcium soaps. Remember to differentiate pigment stones (associated with hemolysis or infection) from cholesterol stones (associated with metabolic factors). Key differentials for RUQ pain include cholecystitis, cholangitis, pancreatitis, hepatitis, peptic ulcer disease, and appendicitis.

<a id='question-011'></a>

### Question 011: Gallbladder and Biliary Tract Pathology: Focus 01-11 (Cholangitis)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Charcot's triad and Reynolds' pentad are clinical presentations associated with acute cholangitis, an infection of the biliary tree, often caused by bacterial obstruction. Understanding the clinical features and common causative organisms is key. (Charcot's triad: fever, RUQ pain, jaundice; Reynolds' pentad: Charcot's triad + altered mental status + shock)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old woman with a history of gallstones presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of worsening right upper quadrant (RUQ) abdominal pain, fever (39°C), and jaundice. She reports chills and nausea. On physical examination, she is found to be tachycardic (110 bpm) and hypotensive (BP 85/50 mmHg). Her sclerae are icteric, and her abdomen is tender to palpation in the RUQ with guarding. Laboratory results show leukocytosis (WBC 22,000/µL with neutrophilia), elevated total bilirubin (8 mg/dL, normal <1.2 mg/dL), elevated alkaline phosphatase (450 U/L, normal 40-120 U/L), and elevated AST/ALT (200 U/L, normal <40 U/L). An abdominal ultrasound reveals dilated common bile duct (1.2 cm diameter) and gallstones in the gallbladder. Given the patient's clinical presentation and laboratory findings, what is the most likely underlying condition?

(A) Acute cholecystitis complicated by perforation
(B) Acute pancreatitis secondary to gallstone obstruction
(C) Acute cholangitis with sepsis
(D) Primary sclerosing cholangitis exacerbation
(E) Hepatocellular carcinoma with biliary obstruction

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old woman with known gallstones (risk factor for biliary obstruction).
- **Symptoms:** RUQ pain, fever, jaundice (Charcot's triad). The duration (3 days) and worsening nature are significant.
- **Vital Signs:** Tachycardia (110 bpm) and hypotension (85/50 mmHg) suggest systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS) or sepsis.
- **Physical Exam:** RUQ tenderness with guarding, icteric sclerae (jaundice).
- **Labs:** Leukocytosis (WBC 22,000/µL) indicates infection/inflammation. Elevated bilirubin (8 mg/dL) and alkaline phosphatase (450 U/L) confirm cholestasis (biliary obstruction). Elevated AST/ALT (200 U/L) suggest some hepatocellular injury, possibly secondary to obstruction or inflammation.
- **Imaging:** Dilated common bile duct (1.2 cm) and gallstones confirm biliary obstruction, likely due to a stone migrating into the common bile duct (choledocholithiasis).
- **Synthesis:** The combination of Charcot's triad (fever, RUQ pain, jaundice) plus signs of systemic toxicity (hypotension, tachycardia, altered mental status is not explicitly stated but hypotension can be a sign of severe sepsis) constitutes Reynolds' pentad, strongly indicative of acute cholangitis complicated by sepsis.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Pathophysiology:** Acute cholangitis is an infection of the biliary tree, typically caused by bacterial ascent from the duodenum or reflux of intestinal contents. Obstruction of the common bile duct (most commonly by gallstones, but also strictures, tumors, parasites) leads to stasis of bile, which promotes bacterial growth. The infection causes inflammation and purulent material within the biliary ducts.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The classic presentation is Charcot's triad (fever, RUQ pain, jaundice). When sepsis develops, it progresses to Reynolds' pentad (Charcot's triad + altered mental status + shock/hypotension).
- **Microbiology:** Common causative organisms include *E. coli*, *Klebsiella*, *Enterococcus*, and *Enterobacter*. Anaerobes like *Bacteroides* can also be involved.
- **Mechanism of Sepsis:** The bacterial infection triggers a systemic inflammatory response, leading to vasodilation, increased capillary permeability, and potentially septic shock (hypotension, organ dysfunction).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Acute cholecystitis complicated by perforation:** While acute cholecystitis (inflammation of the gallbladder) is common with gallstones and presents with RUQ pain and fever, jaundice is less typical unless there is concurrent common bile duct obstruction (Mirizzi syndrome) or perforation leading to peritonitis and secondary biliary obstruction. Perforation usually presents with more severe, generalized peritonitis, not primarily Charcot's triad/Reynolds' pentad. The dilated CBD on ultrasound points away from isolated cholecystitis.
- (B) **Acute pancreatitis secondary to gallstone obstruction:** Gallstones can cause acute pancreatitis by obstructing the ampulla of Vater. This presents with epigastric pain radiating to the back, nausea, vomiting, and elevated amylase/lipase. While RUQ pain can occur, fever and jaundice are less prominent features unless there is concurrent cholangitis or biliary obstruction. The primary lab abnormalities would be elevated pancreatic enzymes, not predominantly cholestatic enzymes (ALP, bilirubin) as seen here.
- (D) **Primary sclerosing cholangitis exacerbation:** PSC is a chronic cholestatic liver disease characterized by inflammation and fibrosis of intrahepatic and extrahepatic bile ducts. It can present with fatigue, pruritus, and jaundice. Acute exacerbations can occur, sometimes triggered by bacterial cholangitis. However, PSC typically has a more insidious onset and is often associated with inflammatory bowel disease. The acute presentation with high fever, severe RUQ pain, and signs of sepsis in a patient with known gallstones makes acute cholangitis secondary to choledocholithiasis much more likely than an exacerbation of underlying PSC (which is not mentioned in the history).
- (E) **Hepatocellular carcinoma with biliary obstruction:** HCC can obstruct the biliary tree, leading to jaundice and RUQ pain. However, fever and signs of sepsis are not typical features of HCC itself unless there is a secondary infection (e.g., cholangitis). HCC usually presents more insidiously, often in patients with underlying cirrhosis. The acute presentation with high fever, leukocytosis, and hypotension strongly points towards an infectious process like cholangitis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Acute Cholangitis (C)                                  | Acute Cholecystitis (A)                               | Acute Pancreatitis (B)                               | PSC Exacerbation (D)                               | HCC with Obstruction (E)                           |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Cause**    | Biliary obstruction + Bacterial infection              | Gallstone obstruction of cystic duct                  | Gallstone obstruction of ampulla of Vater             | Chronic inflammation/fibrosis + possible infection   | Tumor obstruction of bile ducts                      |
| **Classic Triad**    | Fever, RUQ pain, Jaundice (Charcot's)                | RUQ pain, Fever, Nausea/Vomiting (Murphy's sign)      | Epigastric pain (radiates to back), Nausea/Vomiting | Fatigue, Pruritus, Jaundice (often insidious)         | Jaundice, RUQ pain, Weight loss (often insidious)     |
| **Sepsis Signs**     | Common (Reynolds' Pentad: Charcot's + AMS + Shock)   | Uncommon unless perforation/abscess                   | Uncommon                                              | Possible if cholangitis occurs                        | Uncommon unless secondary cholangitis                |
| **Key Labs**         | Leukocytosis, ↑Bilirubin, ↑ALP, ↑AST/ALT              | Leukocytosis, ↑WBC, ↑CRP                             | ↑Amylase, ↑Lipase                                     | ↑ALP, ↑GGT, ↑Bilirubin (often chronic pattern)        | ↑Bilirubin, ↑ALP (may have ↑AFP)                     |
| **Imaging Findings** | Dilated CBD, stones in CBD, air in biliary tree (rare) | Gallbladder wall thickening, pericholecystic fluid   | Normal or signs of pancreatitis (inflammation)        | Strictures, dilatations ("beaded") intra/extrahepatic | Mass lesion, dilated ducts proximal to obstruction |
| **Typical Patient**  | Older, history of gallstones                         | Older, history of gallstones                         | Older, history of gallstones                         | Younger, often with IBD                             | Older, often with cirrhosis                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cholangitis is a bacterial infection of the biliary tree, usually secondary to obstruction (often gallstones). Recognize Charcot's triad (fever, RUQ pain, jaundice) and Reynolds' pentad (Charcot's triad + altered mental status + shock) as key clinical indicators. Differentiate it from acute cholecystitis (primarily gallbladder inflammation, less jaundice), acute pancreatitis (epigastric pain, elevated amylase/lipase), and other causes of biliary obstruction or liver disease.

<a id='question-012'></a>

### Question 012: Gallbladder and Biliary Tract Pathology: Focus 01-12 (CholesterolStones)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the composition and formation mechanism of cholesterol gallstones, differentiating them from pigment stones, and recognizing risk factors. (理解胆固醇胆结石的组成和形成机制，与色素性胆结石区分，并识别风险因素。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old woman presents to the emergency department with severe, constant right upper quadrant pain radiating to her right shoulder blade, nausea, and vomiting for the past 6 hours. She reports similar, milder episodes occurring after fatty meals in the past. Her medical history includes type 2 diabetes mellitus, hyperlipidemia treated with atorvastatin, and obesity (BMI 32 kg/m²). Vital signs show a temperature of 37.2°C, heart rate of 95 bpm, blood pressure of 130/80 mmHg, and respiratory rate of 18 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals tenderness to palpation in the right upper quadrant with a positive Murphy's sign. An abdominal ultrasound shows a gallbladder containing multiple, echogenic stones with posterior acoustic shadowing. The largest stone measures 1.5 cm. Laboratory tests reveal a slightly elevated white blood cell count (12,000/µL) and mildly elevated liver enzymes (AST 45 U/L, ALT 50 U/L). A cholecystectomy is performed, and the gallbladder is sent for pathological examination. Gross examination reveals multiple, round, smooth, pale yellow stones filling the gallbladder lumen. Microscopic examination of a stone fragment shows cholesterol crystals arranged in a radial pattern.

Which of the following is the MOST likely underlying mechanism contributing to the formation of these gallstones?

(A) Increased conjugation of bilirubin with glucuronic acid by UDP-glucuronosyltransferase.
(B) Decreased secretion of bile salts into the bile, leading to decreased cholesterol solubility.
(C) Increased secretion of cholesterol into the bile, exceeding the solubilizing capacity of bile salts and lecithin.
(D) Bacterial infection of the biliary tract leading to increased production of bilirubin from hemoglobin breakdown.
(E) Hemolysis leading to increased unconjugated bilirubin production and precipitation as calcium bilirubinate stones.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old woman with risk factors: female, older age, obesity, type 2 diabetes, hyperlipidemia. These are classic risk factors for cholesterol gallstones (the "5 Fs": Female, Forty, Fat, Fertile, Fair - though "Fair" is less emphasized now).
- **Symptoms:** Acute RUQ pain radiating to the shoulder (biliary colic), nausea, vomiting, positive Murphy's sign – consistent with acute cholecystitis likely secondary to gallstones.
- **Imaging:** Ultrasound showing echogenic stones with acoustic shadowing confirms cholelithiasis.
- **Pathology:** Gross examination shows pale yellow, round, smooth stones. Microscopic examination shows cholesterol crystals in a radial pattern. This morphology is characteristic of cholesterol gallstones.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *underlying mechanism* of formation for *these* specific stones (cholesterol stones).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Cholesterol Gallstone Formation:** Cholesterol gallstones form when bile becomes supersaturated with cholesterol. Cholesterol is normally solubilized in bile by bile salts and lecithin, forming mixed micelles.
- **Supersaturation:** Supersaturation occurs when the concentration of cholesterol exceeds the solubilizing capacity of bile salts and lecithin. This can happen due to:
    - **Increased Cholesterol Secretion:** The liver secretes excessive amounts of cholesterol into the bile. This can be influenced by genetics, diet (high cholesterol, high saturated fat), obesity, and certain medications (e.g., estrogen-containing drugs, fibrates).
    - **Decreased Bile Salt Secretion:** The liver secretes insufficient amounts of bile salts, reducing the solubilizing capacity. This can occur in conditions like ileal disease or resection (where bile salts are reabsorbed) or certain genetic conditions.
    - **Decreased Bile Acid Synthesis:** Impaired synthesis of bile acids in the liver.
- **Nucleation:** Once supersaturated, cholesterol can precipitate out of solution. This process is called nucleation. Mucus glycoproteins in the gallbladder provide a nidus for cholesterol crystal formation.
- **Crystal Aggregation:** Cholesterol monohydrate crystals form and aggregate, eventually forming macroscopic stones.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's risk factors (female, age, obesity, diabetes, hyperlipidemia) are strongly associated with increased hepatic cholesterol secretion into bile, leading to supersaturation and subsequent cholesterol stone formation. The gross and microscopic findings (pale yellow, smooth, cholesterol crystals) confirm the stone type.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Increased conjugation of bilirubin with glucuronic acid by UDP-glucuronosyltransferase leads to increased *conjugated* bilirubin in bile. While impaired bilirubin conjugation (e.g., Gilbert syndrome, Crigler-Najjar) can lead to *unconjugated* hyperbilirubinemia, increased conjugation itself doesn't directly cause cholesterol stones. It might even slightly decrease the risk of pigment stones by clearing bilirubin faster, but it's irrelevant to cholesterol stone formation.
- (B): Decreased secretion of bile salts *does* contribute to cholesterol stone formation by reducing the solubilizing capacity of bile. However, the *primary* driver in many cases, especially with the patient's risk factors, is often *increased* cholesterol secretion exceeding the capacity, even if bile salt secretion is normal or only slightly decreased. Option (C) describes the more common and direct mechanism related to the patient's profile (obesity, hyperlipidemia, diabetes). While decreased bile salt secretion is a mechanism, increased cholesterol secretion is often considered the predominant factor in cholesterol stones, especially in Western populations with high-fat diets.
- (D): Bacterial infection (biliary stasis, inflammation) can lead to the formation of *pigment* stones (calcium bilirubinate), not cholesterol stones. Bacteria like *E. coli* produce beta-glucuronidase, which deconjugates conjugated bilirubin, allowing it to precipitate with calcium. This is more common in developing countries or in patients with chronic biliary infections or stasis. The patient's stones are described as pale yellow and composed of cholesterol crystals, ruling out pigment stones.
- (E): Hemolysis leads to increased production of unconjugated bilirubin. This unconjugated bilirubin is taken up by the liver, conjugated, and excreted into the bile. In conditions of massive hemolysis or impaired liver function, unconjugated bilirubin can precipitate with calcium as *calcium bilirubinate* stones (black pigment stones). This is not the mechanism for the pale yellow cholesterol stones described in the vignette. The patient has no history suggestive of hemolysis (e.g., anemia, jaundice).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Cholesterol Stones                                  | Black Pigment Stones (Calcium Bilirubinate) | Brown Pigment Stones (Calcium Bilirubinate, Mixed) |
|----------------------|----------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|
| **Composition**     | Primarily Cholesterol (>50-70%)                     | Primarily Calcium Bilirubinate (>20%)        | Calcium Bilirubinate, Calcium Salts, Cholesterol |
| **Color**            | Pale Yellow, White                                 | Black                                       | Brown/Dark Brown                                  |
| **Texture**          | Smooth, Hard                                       | Brittle, Friable                             | Soft, Friable                                     |
| **Radiopacity**      | Often radiolucent (may have faint calcification) | Often radiopaque                             | Variable radiopacity                              |
| **Mechanism**        | Cholesterol supersaturation (↑ secretion, ↓ solubility) | ↑ Unconjugated Bilirubin (hemolysis, ineffective erythropoiesis) | Bacterial infection (↑ β-glucuronidase), biliary stasis |
| **Associated Factors**| Obesity, Female, Age, Hyperlipidemia, Diabetes, Estrogens | Hemolytic anemias, cirrhosis, ileal resection | Biliary infection, biliary stasis, parasitic infection |
| **Microscopy**       | Cholesterol crystals (often radial)                 | None or few crystals                         | Bacteria, crystals                                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cholesterol gallstones form due to bile supersaturation with cholesterol, typically driven by increased hepatic cholesterol secretion relative to bile salt secretion. Key risk factors include the "5 Fs" (Female, Forty, Fat, Fertile, Fair). Differentiate cholesterol stones (pale yellow, cholesterol crystals) from pigment stones (black or brown, calcium bilirubinate, associated with hemolysis or infection). Remember that decreased bile salt secretion can also contribute to cholesterol stone formation.

<a id='question-013'></a>

### Question 013: Gallbladder and Biliary Tract Pathology
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the composition and pathogenesis of cholesterol gallstones, differentiating them from pigment stones, and recognizing risk factors. (理解胆固醇胆结石的组成和发病机制，与色素性胆结石区分，并识别风险因素。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old woman presents to the emergency department with severe, constant right upper quadrant pain radiating to her right shoulder blade, nausea, and vomiting. The pain started approximately 2 hours ago after a fatty meal. Her medical history is significant for type 2 diabetes mellitus, hyperlipidemia, and obesity (BMI 35 kg/m²). Vital signs show a temperature of 37.2°C (99.0°F), heart rate of 95 bpm, blood pressure of 130/70 mmHg, and respiratory rate of 18 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals tenderness to palpation in the right upper quadrant with a positive Murphy's sign. Laboratory tests show a slightly elevated white blood cell count (12,000/µL, normal range 4,500-11,000/µL) and mildly elevated liver enzymes (AST 45 U/L, ALT 50 U/L, normal range <40 U/L). An abdominal ultrasound reveals multiple echogenic foci within the gallbladder lumen, with posterior acoustic shadowing, consistent with gallstones. One of the stones is large (approximately 2 cm) and appears layered. Microscopic analysis of a stone obtained during subsequent cholecystectomy shows numerous cholesterol crystals embedded in a matrix of mucin, bile pigments, and calcium salts.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism contributing to the formation of these gallstones?

(A) Increased conjugation of bilirubin leading to precipitation of calcium bilirubinate.
(B) Decreased secretion of bile salts leading to decreased cholesterol solubility.
(C) Increased secretion of cholesterol leading to supersaturation of bile.
(D) Increased activity of bacterial β-glucuronidase leading to increased unconjugated bilirubin.
(E) Decreased secretion of phospholipids leading to increased cholesterol solubility.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old woman with risk factors for cholesterol gallstones: female sex, age > 40, obesity (BMI 35), type 2 diabetes, hyperlipidemia. These are classic "5 Fs" (Female, Forty, Fat, Fertile, Fair - though fertility isn't explicitly mentioned, the other factors are present).
- **Symptoms:** Acute RUQ pain radiating to the shoulder (biliary colic), nausea, vomiting, positive Murphy's sign, suggesting acute cholecystitis or biliary colic due to gallstones.
- **Imaging:** Ultrasound confirms gallstones (echogenic foci with acoustic shadowing). The description of a large, layered stone is characteristic of cholesterol stones.
- **Histopathology:** Microscopic analysis confirms cholesterol crystals as the primary component, embedded in a matrix. This directly points to cholesterol gallstones.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying mechanism of *these* gallstones, which are identified as cholesterol stones based on the clinical picture and microscopic findings.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Cholesterol Gallstone Formation:** The pathogenesis of cholesterol gallstones involves three main factors:
    1.  **Bile Cholesterol Supersaturation:** The concentration of cholesterol in bile exceeds its solubility limit. Cholesterol is normally kept soluble in bile by bile salts and phospholipids (primarily lecithin), which form mixed micelles.
    2.  **Nucleation:** Cholesterol monohydrate crystals begin to form. This process is often facilitated by mucin glycoproteins.
    3.  **Gallbladder Stasis:** Impaired gallbladder emptying allows crystals to aggregate and grow into macroscopic stones.
- **Supersaturation:** Occurs when there is either increased secretion of cholesterol into bile or decreased secretion of bile salts and/or phospholipids. Increased cholesterol secretion can be due to increased hepatic synthesis (driven by factors like obesity, high cholesterol diet, estrogen) or decreased cholesterol clearance. Decreased bile salt/phospholipid secretion can be due to impaired enterohepatic circulation (e.g., ileal disease, rapid weight loss) or decreased synthesis.
- **The patient's risk factors (obesity, diabetes, hyperlipidemia) are strongly associated with increased hepatic cholesterol synthesis and secretion into bile, leading to supersaturation.** The microscopic finding of cholesterol crystals confirms the stone type. Therefore, increased cholesterol secretion leading to supersaturation is the most likely primary mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Increased conjugation of bilirubin leading to precipitation of calcium bilirubinate. This describes the mechanism of *pigment* gallstones (specifically black pigment stones), often associated with chronic hemolysis or cirrhosis. The vignette describes cholesterol stones.
- (B) Decreased secretion of bile salts leading to decreased cholesterol solubility. While decreased bile salt secretion *does* contribute to cholesterol supersaturation (by reducing the solubilizing capacity of bile), the primary driver is often *increased* cholesterol secretion. Furthermore, decreased bile salt secretion leads to *decreased* solubility, which is the opposite of what the option states. The correct mechanism involves decreased solubility *due to supersaturation*, which is caused by increased cholesterol relative to bile salts/phospholipids.
- (D) Increased activity of bacterial β-glucuronidase leading to increased unconjugated bilirubin. This mechanism is primarily associated with *brown* pigment gallstones, typically found in the biliary tract of patients with chronic bacterial infection (e.g., choledocholithiasis, biliary strictures). The vignette describes cholesterol stones forming in the gallbladder.
- (E) Decreased secretion of phospholipids leading to increased cholesterol solubility. Decreased phospholipid secretion (like decreased bile salt secretion) reduces the solubilizing capacity of bile, contributing to cholesterol supersaturation and stone formation. However, the option incorrectly states this leads to *increased* cholesterol solubility. The decreased phospholipids *decrease* the ability of bile to keep cholesterol dissolved, leading to precipitation. Option (C) is a more direct and common primary mechanism, especially given the patient's risk factors pointing towards increased cholesterol secretion.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Cholesterol Stones                                  | Black Pigment Stones                               | Brown Pigment Stones                               |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Component**   | Cholesterol monohydrate crystals                    | Calcium bilirubinate                                | Calcium bilirubinate, cholesterol, bacteria         |
| **Appearance**          | Often large, layered, yellow/tan                     | Small, brittle, black                               | Soft, greasy, brown/black                           |
| **Location**            | Gallbladder (most common)                           | Gallbladder, common bile duct                       | Biliary tract (often with infection/stasis)         |
| **Underlying Mechanism** | Bile cholesterol supersaturation, nucleation, stasis | Increased unconjugated bilirubin (hemolysis, cirrhosis) | Bacterial β-glucuronidase activity (infection)      |
| **Associated Conditions**| Obesity, female sex, age, hyperlipidemia, diabetes | Chronic hemolysis, cirrhosis                        | Chronic biliary infection, strictures, parasites     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cholesterol gallstones form due to bile cholesterol supersaturation, nucleation, and gallbladder stasis. Risk factors include the "5 Fs" (Female, Forty, Fat, Fertile, Fair). Pigment stones (black or brown) have different mechanisms involving bilirubin metabolism or bacterial enzymes. Differentiating stone types is crucial for understanding pathogenesis and potential management strategies.

---

<a id='question-014'></a>

### Question 014: Gallbladder and Biliary Tract Pathology: Focus 01-14 (increases with age. Risk factors include female ge)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis and histopathological features of gallstones (cholelithiasis), specifically cholesterol stones, and differentiating them from other types of gallstones or biliary pathologies. (理解胆结石（胆石症）的发病机制和组织病理学特征，特别是胆固醇结石，并将其与其他类型的胆结石或胆道病理区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old woman with a history of obesity, type 2 diabetes mellitus, and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute, severe right upper quadrant abdominal pain radiating to her right shoulder. The pain started approximately 2 hours ago after a fatty meal. Her vital signs show a temperature of 37.8°C (100.0°F), heart rate of 110 bpm, blood pressure of 130/80 mmHg, and respiratory rate of 18 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals marked tenderness to palpation in the right upper quadrant with a positive Murphy's sign. Laboratory tests show a slightly elevated white blood cell count (12,000/µL, normal range 4,500-11,000/µL) and elevated liver enzymes (AST 85 U/L, ALT 90 U/L, normal range <40 U/L). An abdominal ultrasound reveals multiple echogenic foci within the gallbladder lumen, with posterior acoustic shadowing, consistent with gallstones. One stone measures 1.8 cm in diameter. A subsequent cholecystectomy is performed. Gross examination of the gallbladder shows a distended organ containing numerous stones. Histopathological examination of the gallbladder wall reveals chronic cholecystitis with focal areas of acute inflammation and necrosis. The stones are predominantly yellow-white, friable, and appear to be composed primarily of cholesterol crystals.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism for the formation of the predominant type of gallstones observed in this patient?

(A) Increased biliary excretion of unconjugated bilirubin leading to precipitation of calcium bilirubinate.
(B) Supersaturation of bile with cholesterol due to decreased bile salt synthesis and increased cholesterol secretion.
(C) Bacterial infection of the biliary tree causing deconjugation of bilirubin and precipitation of calcium bilirubinate.
(D) Hemolysis leading to increased bilirubin production and precipitation of calcium bilirubinate.
(E) Decreased biliary excretion of cholesterol due to impaired function of the cystic duct.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old woman with obesity, type 2 diabetes, and hyperlipidemia. These are classic risk factors for cholesterol gallstones (the "4 Fs": Female, Forty, Fat, Fertile - although fertility isn't explicitly mentioned, age and obesity are strong indicators).
*   **Symptoms:** Acute RUQ pain radiating to the shoulder after a fatty meal, positive Murphy's sign, elevated WBC, and elevated liver enzymes are consistent with acute cholecystitis secondary to gallstones (biliary colic progressing to cholecystitis).
*   **Imaging:** Ultrasound confirms gallstones (echogenic foci with acoustic shadowing).
*   **Gross Pathology:** Multiple stones in a distended gallbladder.
*   **Histopathology:** Chronic cholecystitis with acute inflammation/necrosis.
*   **Stone Description:** Yellow-white, friable stones composed primarily of cholesterol crystals. This is the key description pointing towards cholesterol stones.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying mechanism of the *predominant type* of gallstones (cholesterol stones) in this patient.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Cholesterol Gallstone Formation:** Cholesterol stones form when bile becomes supersaturated with cholesterol. This supersaturation occurs due to an imbalance between cholesterol secretion into bile and the solubilizing capacity of bile salts and phospholipids (primarily lecithin).
*   **Mechanism:**
    *   **Increased Cholesterol Secretion:** The liver secretes more cholesterol into bile. This can be due to increased hepatic cholesterol synthesis (often linked to obesity, diabetes, hyperlipidemia) or increased cholesterol esterification within hepatocytes.
    *   **Decreased Bile Salt Synthesis:** Bile salts are crucial for keeping cholesterol solubilized in bile (forming mixed micelles). Reduced bile salt synthesis (e.g., due to genetic factors, certain medications, or ileal disease affecting bile salt reabsorption) decreases cholesterol solubility.
    *   **Decreased Phospholipid Secretion:** Lecithin also helps solubilize cholesterol. Reduced secretion decreases solubility.
    *   **Stasis:** Gallbladder stasis (e.g., due to pregnancy, rapid weight loss, prolonged fasting, TPN) allows cholesterol crystals to precipitate and aggregate.
*   **Patient's Risk Factors:** The patient's obesity, diabetes, and hyperlipidemia are strongly associated with increased hepatic cholesterol synthesis and secretion, contributing to bile supersaturation with cholesterol.
*   **Other Stone Types:**
    *   **Pigment Stones (Black):** Composed primarily of calcium bilirubinate. Associated with chronic hemolysis (increased unconjugated bilirubin) or cirrhosis (impaired bilirubin excretion).
    *   **Pigment Stones (Brown):** Composed primarily of calcium bilirubinate and cholesterol. Associated with biliary infection (bacterial β-glucuronidase deconjugates bilirubin) and stasis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Increased biliary excretion of unconjugated bilirubin leading to precipitation of calcium bilirubinate. This describes the mechanism for *black pigment stones*, typically seen in chronic hemolytic anemias or cirrhosis. The patient's stones are described as yellow-white and cholesterol-rich, not black pigment stones.
- (C): Bacterial infection of the biliary tree causing deconjugation of bilirubin and precipitation of calcium bilirubinate. This describes the mechanism for *brown pigment stones*, typically associated with biliary stasis and infection (e.g., choledocholithiasis, biliary strictures). While the patient has inflammation, the primary stone type is cholesterol, not brown pigment.
- (D): Hemolysis leading to increased bilirubin production and precipitation of calcium bilirubinate. Similar to (A), this describes the mechanism for *black pigment stones* due to increased unconjugated bilirubin from hemolysis. The patient has no history or findings suggestive of hemolysis, and the stones are cholesterol-based.
- (E): Decreased biliary excretion of cholesterol due to impaired function of the cystic duct. Impaired cystic duct function leads to gallbladder stasis, which *promotes* the precipitation and aggregation of cholesterol crystals *already present* in supersaturated bile. It doesn't directly cause the initial supersaturation itself. The primary issue in cholesterol stone formation is the imbalance leading to supersaturation (increased cholesterol secretion relative to bile salts/phospholipids).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Cholesterol Stones                                  | Black Pigment Stones                        | Brown Pigment Stones                       |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------------- |
| **Composition**         | Primarily Cholesterol (>50-70%)                      | Primarily Calcium Bilirubinate (>20%)       | Calcium Bilirubinate, Cholesterol, Bacteria |
| **Color**               | Yellow-white, often friable                         | Black, hard                                 | Soft, greasy, brown/green                  |
| **Associated Conditions** | Obesity, Diabetes, Hyperlipidemia, Female, Age, Rapid Weight Loss | Chronic Hemolysis, Cirrhosis                | Biliary Infection, Stasis                  |
| **Mechanism**           | Bile supersaturation with cholesterol               | Increased unconjugated bilirubin             | Bacterial deconjugation of bilirubin        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The most common type of gallstone in Western countries is the cholesterol stone, driven by bile supersaturation with cholesterol due to increased secretion and/or decreased solubilization. Key risk factors include the "4 Fs" (Female, Forty, Fat, Fertile) and metabolic syndrome components like obesity, diabetes, and hyperlipidemia. Differentiate cholesterol stones from pigment stones (black vs. brown) based on composition, color, associated conditions, and underlying mechanisms.

<a id='question-015'></a>

### Question 015: Gallbladder and Biliary Tract Pathology
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the association between exogenous estrogen exposure (oral contraceptives) and the development of gallbladder adenomatosis, specifically focusing on the underlying mechanism involving estrogen receptors and cellular proliferation. (考察外源性雌激素暴露（口服避孕药）与胆囊腺瘤增生发展之间的关联，重点是雌激素受体和细胞增殖的潜在机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old female presents to the clinic complaining of intermittent right upper quadrant abdominal pain, often exacerbated after fatty meals. She has been taking combined oral contraceptives (containing ethinylestradiol and levonorgestrel) for the past 10 years for contraception. Her medical history is otherwise unremarkable. Physical examination reveals mild tenderness in the right upper quadrant upon palpation, but no guarding or rebound tenderness. Laboratory tests, including liver function tests (AST, ALT, alkaline phosphatase, bilirubin) and complete blood count, are within normal limits. An abdominal ultrasound shows a normal-sized gallbladder with a thickened wall (6 mm) and multiple small, polypoid lesions (up to 5 mm) projecting into the lumen. Cholecystectomy is performed. Gross examination of the gallbladder reveals diffuse adenomatosis, characterized by numerous small, tubular adenomas lining the gallbladder mucosa. Microscopic examination confirms the presence of these adenomas, which are composed of columnar epithelial cells with increased nuclear-cytoplasmic ratio and mild atypia. Immunohistochemical staining shows strong, diffuse positivity for estrogen receptors (ER) and progesterone receptors (PR) within the adenomatous epithelium.

Which of the following is the most likely mechanism underlying the development of gallbladder adenomatosis in this patient?

(A) Chronic inflammation leading to metaplasia and dysplasia of the gallbladder epithelium, driven by bacterial infection.
(B) Genetic mutation in the *APC* gene, leading to uncontrolled cell proliferation and adenoma formation, similar to familial adenomatous polyposis (FAP).
(C) Prolonged exposure to exogenous estrogen, stimulating proliferation of gallbladder epithelial cells expressing estrogen receptors.
(D) Chronic obstruction of the cystic duct leading to bile stasis, inflammation, and subsequent epithelial changes, including adenoma formation.
(E) Activation of the *KRAS* oncogene due to chronic cholecystitis, resulting in constitutive activation of downstream signaling pathways promoting cell growth.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old female, long-term (10 years) user of combined oral contraceptives (containing ethinylestradiol, a synthetic estrogen). This immediately raises suspicion for estrogen-related effects.
*   **Symptoms:** Intermittent RUQ pain, worse after fatty meals, suggestive of biliary colic or gallbladder pathology.
*   **Imaging:** Ultrasound shows gallbladder wall thickening and multiple small polyps.
*   **Pathology:** Gross examination reveals diffuse adenomatosis (numerous small tubular adenomas). Microscopic examination confirms adenomas with mild atypia.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** Strong, diffuse positivity for estrogen receptors (ER) and progesterone receptors (PR) in the adenomatous epithelium. This is a crucial clue linking the pathology to hormonal influence.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the *mechanism* underlying the adenomatosis.

The combination of long-term oral contraceptive use (exogenous estrogen), the specific pathology of diffuse gallbladder adenomatosis, and the immunohistochemical finding of strong ER positivity strongly points towards estrogen as the driving factor.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Gallbladder Adenomatosis:** A condition characterized by the presence of numerous small adenomas (typically < 1 cm) throughout the gallbladder mucosa. It is often associated with long-term use of oral contraceptives or hormone replacement therapy.
*   **Estrogen Receptors:** Nuclear receptors that bind estrogen hormones (like estradiol). Upon binding, they translocate to the nucleus and act as transcription factors, regulating the expression of target genes involved in cell proliferation, differentiation, and apoptosis.
*   **Mechanism:** Exogenous estrogen (from oral contraceptives) binds to estrogen receptors (ERα and ERβ) present on gallbladder epithelial cells. This binding stimulates intracellular signaling pathways (e.g., MAPK, PI3K/Akt) that promote cell proliferation and inhibit apoptosis, leading to the formation of adenomas. The diffuse nature of the adenomatosis suggests a widespread effect of the hormone on the gallbladder epithelium. The immunohistochemical confirmation of ER expression in the adenomas directly supports this mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Chronic inflammation leading to metaplasia and dysplasia... bacterial infection.** While chronic inflammation (cholecystitis) can lead to gallbladder cancer, it typically involves squamous metaplasia or intestinal metaplasia, followed by dysplasia and carcinoma. Bacterial infection is not the primary driver of adenomatosis, especially diffuse adenomatosis linked to hormonal exposure. The pathology described (adenomatosis) and the hormonal context make this incorrect.
- (B): **Genetic mutation in the *APC* gene... similar to familial adenomatous polyposis (FAP).** *APC* mutations are characteristic of colorectal adenomas and FAP. While adenomas can occur in the gallbladder, *APC* mutations are not the typical cause of diffuse gallbladder adenomatosis associated with oral contraceptives. FAP is a distinct genetic syndrome primarily affecting the colon.
- (D): **Chronic obstruction of the cystic duct... bile stasis, inflammation... epithelial changes... adenoma formation.** Chronic cystic duct obstruction leads to chronic cholecystitis, hydrops, and potentially gallbladder cancer, often associated with squamous metaplasia. It does not typically cause diffuse adenomatosis, and the primary mechanism is not hormonal stimulation. The patient's labs were normal, making significant obstruction less likely.
- (E): **Activation of the *KRAS* oncogene... chronic cholecystitis... constitutive activation... cell growth.** *KRAS* mutations are common in various cancers, including pancreatic and colorectal cancer, and can occur in gallbladder cancer, often in the context of chronic inflammation. However, *KRAS* activation is not the primary mechanism specifically linked to estrogen-induced gallbladder adenomatosis. The strong ER positivity points directly to a hormonal mechanism.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Gallbladder Adenomatosis (Estrogen-Related) | Chronic Cholecystitis/Obstruction | Colorectal Adenoma (FAP) | Gallbladder Carcinoma (Inflammation-Related) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :-------------------------------- | :----------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- |
| **Etiology**            | Exogenous estrogen (OCPs, HRT)              | Cystic duct obstruction, infection | *APC* gene mutation      | Chronic inflammation, stones, infection        |
| **Pathology**           | Diffuse small tubular adenomas               | Inflammation, fibrosis, metaplasia | Tubular adenomas         | Dysplasia, carcinoma (various types)          |
| **Key Finding**         | Strong ER/PR positivity                     | Squamous metaplasia, fibrosis     | *APC* loss              | Dysplasia, invasion, metastasis               |
| **Associated Condition** | OCP/HRT use                                 | Gallstones, biliary sludge        | FAP                    | Chronic cholecystitis, porcelain gallbladder   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Long-term exposure to exogenous estrogens (e.g., from oral contraceptives or hormone replacement therapy) is a known risk factor for the development of diffuse gallbladder adenomatosis. The underlying mechanism involves estrogen binding to estrogen receptors on gallbladder epithelial cells, stimulating proliferation. Key differentials include gallbladder polyps (adenomatous, inflammatory, cholesterol), chronic cholecystitis, and gallbladder carcinoma. Remember to associate hormonal exposure with ER/PR positivity in gallbladder adenomatosis.

<a id='question-016'></a>

### Question 016: Gallbladder and Biliary Tract Pathology
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the genetic predisposition and molecular pathogenesis of specific biliary tract cancers, particularly cholangiocarcinoma, in specific ethnic populations. (理解特定族裔中胆管癌的遗传易感性和分子发病机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old Navajo woman presents to her primary care physician with a 3-month history of worsening jaundice, pruritus, and fatigue. She denies abdominal pain, fever, or weight loss. Her past medical history is significant for type 2 diabetes mellitus, well-controlled with metformin. She has no history of gallstones or prior biliary surgery. Family history is notable for a brother who died of liver cancer at age 62. Physical examination reveals scleral icterus and mild hepatomegaly. Laboratory studies show elevated total bilirubin (8.5 mg/dL, normal <1.2 mg/dL), alkaline phosphatase (ALP) (650 U/L, normal 30-120 U/L), and GGT (300 U/L, normal <60 U/L). AST and ALT are mildly elevated (2x upper limit of normal). Abdominal ultrasound reveals intrahepatic biliary ductal dilation without evidence of gallstones. Subsequent MRCP confirms the dilation and identifies a stricture in the common hepatic duct. Endoscopic retrograde cholangiopancreatography (ERCP) with brush cytology of the stricture reveals malignant cells. Biopsy of the stricture shows moderately differentiated adenocarcinoma infiltrating the bile duct epithelium, with desmoplastic stroma. Immunohistochemical staining is positive for CK7 and CK19, and negative for CK20 and hepatocyte antigen.

Which of the following is the MOST likely underlying genetic predisposition contributing to this patient's condition?

(A) Mutations in the *BRCA1* or *BRCA2* genes, commonly associated with hereditary breast and ovarian cancer syndrome.
(B) Mutations in the *APC* gene, associated with familial adenomatous polyposis (FAP).
(C) Mutations in the *FGFR2* gene, associated with Choledochal cysts.
(D) Mutations in the *KRAS* gene, commonly found in pancreatic adenocarcinoma.
(E) Mutations in the *TP53* gene, associated with Li-Fraumeni syndrome.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Demographics:** 58-year-old Navajo woman. This ethnicity is a crucial clue, as certain populations have higher risks for specific diseases.
*   **Symptoms:** Jaundice, pruritus, fatigue (suggestive of biliary obstruction).
*   **History:** Type 2 diabetes (risk factor for some cancers), no gallstones/surgery (less likely primary sclerosing cholangitis or gallstone-related issues). Family history of liver cancer (suggests potential genetic predisposition).
*   **Physical Exam:** Scleral icterus, hepatomegaly (consistent with biliary obstruction).
*   **Labs:** Elevated bilirubin, ALP, GGT (cholestatic pattern), mildly elevated AST/ALT (suggests biliary obstruction rather than primary hepatocellular injury).
*   **Imaging:** Ultrasound and MRCP show intrahepatic biliary dilation and a common hepatic duct stricture (suggests obstruction, potentially malignant).
*   **ERCP/Cytology/Biopsy:** Malignant cells, adenocarcinoma infiltrating bile duct epithelium, desmoplastic stroma (confirms cholangiocarcinoma).
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** CK7+, CK19+, CK20-, Hepatocyte antigen- (typical profile for cholangiocarcinoma, distinguishing it from hepatocellular carcinoma or metastatic adenocarcinoma from other sites).
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely *underlying genetic predisposition* contributing to this patient's cholangiocarcinoma, given her ethnicity and clinical presentation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Cholangiocarcinoma:** Cancer arising from the epithelial cells of the bile ducts. Can be intrahepatic or extrahepatic.
*   **Risk Factors:** Primary sclerosing cholangitis (PSC), liver fluke infection (Opisthorchis viverrini, Clonorchis sinensis), choledochal cysts, hepatolithiasis, chronic biliary inflammation, cirrhosis, diabetes mellitus, obesity, and certain genetic syndromes.
*   **Ethnic Predisposition:** Navajo Indians (and other Native American populations) have a significantly increased incidence of cholangiocarcinoma compared to the general population.
*   **Genetic Link:** This increased risk in Navajo populations is strongly associated with mutations in the *FGFR2* gene (Fibroblast Growth Factor Receptor 2). These mutations are also the underlying cause of Caroli disease and autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease (ADPKD), which are often associated with choledochal cysts. Choledochal cysts themselves are a major risk factor for cholangiocarcinoma, and the *FGFR2* mutations predispose to both the cysts and the subsequent cancer. The *FGFR2* gene encodes a receptor tyrosine kinase involved in cell growth, differentiation, and migration. Gain-of-function mutations in *FGFR2* lead to abnormal bile duct development (choledochal cysts) and increased susceptibility to malignant transformation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Mutations in the *BRCA1* or *BRCA2* genes:** These genes are tumor suppressor genes involved in DNA repair. Mutations significantly increase the risk of breast, ovarian, prostate, and pancreatic cancers, but are not the primary genetic predisposition associated with cholangiocarcinoma, especially in the Navajo population.
*   **(B) Mutations in the *APC* gene:** This gene is a tumor suppressor gene involved in the Wnt signaling pathway. Germline mutations cause familial adenomatous polyposis (FAP), characterized by numerous colorectal adenomas and a very high risk of colorectal cancer. While *APC* mutations can occur somatically in various cancers, they are not the specific genetic predisposition linked to cholangiocarcinoma in Navajo individuals.
*   **(D) Mutations in the *KRAS* gene:** *KRAS* is an oncogene frequently mutated in pancreatic adenocarcinoma, colorectal cancer, and lung cancer. While *KRAS* mutations can occur in cholangiocarcinoma, they are not the specific genetic predisposition strongly associated with the increased risk in the Navajo population.
*   **(E) Mutations in the *TP53* gene:** This gene is a critical tumor suppressor gene, often called the "guardian of the genome." Germline mutations cause Li-Fraumeni syndrome, predisposing individuals to a wide range of cancers, including sarcomas, breast cancer, brain tumors, leukemia, and adrenal cortical carcinoma. While *TP53* mutations are common in many cancers, including cholangiocarcinoma, they are not the specific genetic factor linked to the high incidence in Navajo populations.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Cholangiocarcinoma (Navajo) | BRCA1/2 Mutations | APC Mutations (FAP) | KRAS Mutations (Pancreatic Ca) | TP53 Mutations (Li-Fraumeni) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------- | :---------------- | :----------------- | :----------------------------- | :--------------------------- |
| **Primary Cancer Risk** | Cholangiocarcinoma         | Breast, Ovarian   | Colorectal        | Pancreatic                    | Wide range (Sarcoma, Breast, etc.) |
| **Specific Population** | Navajo Indians             | General Population | General Population | General Population             | General Population           |
| **Associated Gene**     | *FGFR2*                    | *BRCA1*, *BRCA2*   | *APC*              | *KRAS*                        | *TP53*                      |
| **Pathophysiology Link**| Choledochal Cysts          | DNA Repair Defect | Wnt Signaling Defect | Oncogene Activation           | DNA Repair/Apoptosis Defect |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Recognize that specific ethnic populations have distinct genetic predispositions to certain cancers. In Navajo Indians, mutations in the *FGFR2* gene are strongly associated with an increased risk of cholangiocarcinoma, often mediated through the development of choledochal cysts. Differentiate this specific genetic link from other common cancer predisposition syndromes like BRCA1/2, FAP, Li-Fraumeni, and common oncogene mutations like KRAS.

<a id='question-017'></a>

### Question 017: Gallbladder and Biliary Tract Pathology: Focus 01-17 (Pigmented BilirubinateStones)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology and composition of pigmented gallstones, specifically black pigment stones, and their association with chronic hemolytic conditions leading to increased unconjugated bilirubin. (问题考察的是色素性胆结石，特别是黑色色素性结石的病理生理机制及其与慢性溶血性疾病导致未结合胆红素增多的关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old African American woman with a known history of sickle cell anemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, intermittent right upper quadrant abdominal pain radiating to her back for the past 24 hours. She reports nausea and vomiting but denies fever or jaundice. Her vital signs show a temperature of 37.2°C (99.0°F), heart rate of 110 bpm, blood pressure of 130/80 mmHg, and respiratory rate of 18 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals tenderness to palpation in the right upper quadrant with a positive Murphy's sign. Laboratory results show a hemoglobin of 7.5 g/dL (normal range: 12-16 g/dL), MCV of 105 fL (normal range: 80-100 fL), reticulocyte count of 15% (normal range: 0.5-2.5%), total bilirubin of 3.0 mg/dL (normal range: 0.3-1.2 mg/dL), direct bilirubin of 0.5 mg/dL (normal range: 0.0-0.3 mg/dL), AST of 45 U/L (normal range: 10-40 U/L), ALT of 50 U/L (normal range: 7-56 U/L), and alkaline phosphatase of 150 U/L (normal range: 44-147 U/L). An abdominal ultrasound reveals multiple echogenic foci within the gallbladder lumen, consistent with gallstones, and mild gallbladder wall thickening. Which of the following is the most likely composition of the gallstones in this patient?

(A) Primarily cholesterol crystals with minimal calcium bilirubinate.
(B) Primarily calcium bilirubinate with minimal cholesterol.
(C) Primarily cholesterol crystals with significant calcium bilirubinate and calcium phosphate.
(D) Primarily calcium bilirubinate with significant cholesterol and calcium phosphate.
(E) Primarily calcium bilirubinate with significant cholesterol and calcium stearate.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old African American woman with sickle cell anemia. Sickle cell anemia is a chronic hemolytic condition.
- **Symptoms:** Right upper quadrant pain, nausea, vomiting, positive Murphy's sign – classic symptoms of acute cholecystitis or biliary colic, suggesting gallstones.
- **Labs:**
    - Low Hemoglobin (7.5 g/dL) and high Reticulocyte count (15%) indicate ongoing hemolysis.
    - High MCV (105 fL) is consistent with macrocytosis often seen in hemolytic anemias due to increased reticulocytes.
    - Elevated Total Bilirubin (3.0 mg/dL) with predominantly Indirect/Unconjugated Bilirubin (Direct bilirubin 0.5 mg/dL, Indirect = Total - Direct = 2.5 mg/dL) is characteristic of hemolytic conditions where red blood cell breakdown releases large amounts of hemoglobin, which is metabolized to unconjugated bilirubin.
    - Mildly elevated AST, ALT, and Alkaline Phosphatase suggest some degree of biliary obstruction or inflammation, but are not dramatically high.
- **Imaging:** Ultrasound confirms gallstones.
- **Question:** Asks for the most likely composition of the gallstones.
- **Connecting Clues:** The patient has a chronic hemolytic condition (sickle cell anemia) leading to increased production of unconjugated bilirubin. Chronic hemolysis is a major risk factor for the formation of black pigment gallstones.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Gallstone Formation:** Gallstones form when bile becomes supersaturated with either cholesterol or bilirubin salts, or when gallbladder motility is impaired.
- **Cholesterol Stones:** These are the most common type (80-90% in Western countries). They form when bile contains too much cholesterol relative to bile salts and lecithin. Risk factors include obesity, female sex, age, rapid weight loss, certain medications (e.g., estrogen), and metabolic syndrome. They are typically yellow or greenish.
- **Pigment Stones:** These are less common (10-20%) and are composed primarily of calcium bilirubinate. They are further divided into black and brown pigment stones.
    - **Black Pigment Stones:** Composed primarily of calcium bilirubinate polymers, often with calcium phosphate and calcium carbonate. They form in sterile bile, typically in conditions associated with chronic hemolysis (e.g., sickle cell anemia, hereditary spherocytosis, thalassemia) or cirrhosis. The increased unconjugated bilirubin from hemolysis is converted to calcium bilirubinate in the gallbladder, which precipitates out of solution. These stones are often small, multiple, and brittle.
    - **Brown Pigment Stones:** Composed of calcium bilirubinate, cholesterol, and calcium soaps (like calcium stearate). They form in infected bile, often associated with biliary tract infections (e.g., bacteria like E. coli produce beta-glucuronidase, which deconjugates bilirubin conjugates, increasing unconjugated bilirubin) or biliary stasis. They are often large, soft, and greasy.
- **Patient's Case:** The patient has sickle cell anemia, a chronic hemolytic condition. This leads to increased unconjugated bilirubin. The ultrasound shows gallstones. Given the underlying hemolytic anemia, the most likely type of gallstones is black pigment stones, which are primarily composed of calcium bilirubinate.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Primarily cholesterol crystals with minimal calcium bilirubinate. This describes typical cholesterol stones, which are associated with cholesterol supersaturation, not chronic hemolysis. Incorrect.
- (C): Primarily cholesterol crystals with significant calcium bilirubinate and calcium phosphate. This suggests a mixed stone type, potentially brown pigment stones (which contain cholesterol) or cholesterol stones with some pigment. However, the strong association with chronic hemolysis points more strongly towards pigment stones as the primary component. While some cholesterol might be present, the primary composition in hemolytic states is calcium bilirubinate. Incorrect.
- (D): Primarily calcium bilirubinate with significant cholesterol and calcium phosphate. This composition is more characteristic of brown pigment stones, which form in the setting of biliary infection or stasis, not typically chronic hemolysis. Incorrect.
- (E): Primarily calcium bilirubinate with significant cholesterol and calcium stearate. Calcium stearate is a component often found in brown pigment stones due to bacterial action (beta-glucuronidase activity). This composition is associated with infection, not chronic hemolysis. Incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Cholesterol Stones                  | Black Pigment Stones (Ca Bilirubinate) | Brown Pigment Stones (Ca Bilirubinate, Cholesterol, Ca Soaps) |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Component** | Cholesterol                          | Calcium Bilirubinate                  | Calcium Bilirubinate, Cholesterol, Calcium Soaps            |
| **Associated Condition**| Obesity, Female, Age, Rapid Weight Loss | Chronic Hemolysis, Cirrhosis         | Biliary Infection, Stasis                                   |
| **Bile Environment** | Cholesterol Supersaturation          | Sterile Bile                          | Infected Bile                                                |
| **Color**            | Yellow/Green                         | Black                                 | Brown/Green                                                 |
| **Texture**          | Hard                                 | Brittle, Small, Multiple              | Soft, Large, Greasy                                          |
| **Patient Example**  | Obese female                       | Sickle Cell Anemia                    | Ascending Cholangitis                                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Chronic hemolytic conditions (like sickle cell anemia, thalassemia, hereditary spherocytosis) lead to increased unconjugated bilirubin, which precipitates as calcium bilirubinate in the gallbladder, forming black pigment gallstones. Differentiate these from cholesterol stones (most common, associated with metabolic factors) and brown pigment stones (associated with biliary infection).
